    In the soundness analysis we will find some zoom-in and zoom-out pair (Q, W ) for the larger prover,
and we will be interested in projecting it into a question V of the smaller prover. In particular, we will be
interested in the distribution of the codimension W ∩ FVq . The following consequence of Lemma 5.8 upper
bounds the probability its codimension is smaller by j than the codimension of W .

Lemma 5.9. For integer r, s ⩾ 0, the following holds for sufficiently large k. Let U be a fixed question to
the first prover in the outer PCP consisting of 3k distinct variables in some set of k equations, let Q ⊆ FU
                                                                                                           q
be a subspace of dimension r, and let W ⊆ FU  q  be a codimension   s subspace that contains both  H U and Q.
Let V ⊆ U be a random question to the second prover chosen according to the outer PCP, conditioned on
Q ⊆ FVq , and let W [V ] = FVq ∩ W . Then, for each 0 ⩽ j ⩽ s, we have

                                                 Pr      [codim(W [V ]) = s − j] ⩽ (2sβq 3r )j ,
                                           V ∼A(·,Q)


where the codimension is with respect to FVq .

Proof. Let us label the variables in U as (x1 , . . . , x3k ) so that the entries of vectors in FU        q are indexed
by variables in (x1 , . . . , x3k ). Recall that the equations making up U are labeled as e1 , . . . , ek . We will
choose V gradually, where at each step, we choose a new equation from {e1 , . . . , ek } and then choose
which variables to drop from it according to the sampling procedure of the outer PCP. Specifically, at step
i, let e′1 , . . . , e′i−1 ∈ {e1 , . . . , ek } be the equations that have been processed already. We choose e′i ∈
{e1 , . . . , ek } \ {e′1 , . . . , e′i−1 }. Then, with probability 1 − β we do not drop any variables from e′i , and with
probability β we drop a uniformly random two out of the three variables from e′i . Recall that when a variable
is dropped when obtaining V , every vector in FVq has coordinate zero at the index of the dropped variable.
      Let V0 = U and let Vi be the state of V after step i in this process, i.e. after we have processed equations
e1 , . . . , e′i ∈ {e1 , . . . , ek } and chosen which variables to drop from these equations. Also define W [Vi ] :=
 ′

W ∩ FVq i and say that codim(W [Vi ]) is the codimension of W [Vi ] with respect to FVq i . We will first show

                                                                              30
that it is possible to adaptively choose the first k − s equations so that the codimension does not drop, i.e.
codim(W [V0 ]) = · · · = codim(W [Vk−s ]). For each one of the remaining s steps we will (roughly) show
that the codimension drops by 1 with probability at most β, and does not change with probability 1 − β.
    At step i, call an equation e saturated if the restriction of W [Vi−1 ] to the variables in e has dimension 3.
If we choose e′i = e, it is straightforward to check that codim(W [Vi ]) = codim(W [Vi−1 ]) with probability
1. We argue inductively that for the first k − s steps, we can always adaptively choose a saturated equation,
and thus have codim(W [V0 ]) = · · · = codim(W [Vk−s ]).
    Fix an arbitrary i such that 1 ⩽ i ⩽ k − s and suppose codim(W [Vi−1 ]) = s. We claim that there
can be at most s unsaturated equations in {e1 , . . . , ek } \ {e′1 , . . . , e′i−1 }. To see this, note that for each
equation e ∈ {e1 , . . . , ek } \ {e′1 , . . . , e′i−1 } that is unsaturated there is a corresponding vector in the dual
                          V
space of W [Vi−1 ] ⊂ Fq i−1 supported only on the indices corresponding to variables in e. Thus, for any set
of distinct unsaturated equations, these vectors in the dual space are linearly independent. Since the dual
space of W [Vi−1 ] has dimension at most s, there are thus at most s unsaturated equations. Therefore, if
1 ⩽ i ⩽ k − s, then there is always at least one saturated equation in {e1 , . . . , ek } \ {e′1 , . . . , e′i−1 }, and we
can adaptively choose e′i to be this equation.
    We now show that for the unsaturated equations, the codimension can drop by 1 with probability at most
     β
(1−β)q −3r
            and otherwise the codimension does not change. Let ei be an equation that is unsaturated. By
                                               β
Lemma 5.8, with probability at least 1 − (1−β)q   −3r no variables in ei are dropped and in this case we have

Vi = Vi+1 , W [Vi ] = W [Vi+1 ], and hence codim(W [Vi ]) = codim(W [Vi+1 ]).
                                      β
    With the remaining at most (1−β)q   −3r probability, two variables are dropped, and in this case we show
that the codimension decreases by at most 1. Suppose a1 , a2 are the characteristic vectors for the variables
that are dropped. Recall that HU ⊆ W at the start, and let wi be the coefficient vector of the ith equation so
that wi ∈ HU . Then, wi ∈ W [Vi ], so it must be the case that a1 is not yet in the dual space of W [Vi ], and so,
when viewing both W [Vi+1 ] and W [Vi ] as subspaces of FVq i , we get that dim(W [Vi ]) − dim(W [Vi+1 ]) ⩾ 1.
                                                              
                                                          V
Combining this with the fact that dim FVq i − dim Fq i+1 = 2, where again both spaces are viewed as
                                             

                                                                                   V
subspaces of FVq i it follows that the codimension of W [Vi+1 ] relative to Fq i+1 is at most one more than that
of W [Vi ] relative to FVq i .
    Altogether, this shows that for k − s of the steps i, the codimension does not change, while for the
                                                                                    β
remaining s steps, the codimension drops by 1 with probability at most (1−β)q         −3r and remains the same
otherwise. As a result,
                                                                          j
                                                         s            β
                      Pr [codim(W [V ]) = s − j] ⩽                                ⩽ (2sβq 3r )j ,
                   V ∼A(·,Q)                             j     (1 − β)q −3r

where the second inequality is by union bounding over all possible combinations of j out of s steps where
the codimension may drop by 1.

    We finish this section by stating a corollary of Lemma 5.9 which will be used in the soundness analysis.
The setting one should have in mind is that we have a zoom-in and zoom-out pair (Q, W ) and an event
L ⊆ (FU  q )

5.3     The Number of Maximal Zoom-Outs is Bounded
Theorem 5.2 suggests that the two provers can agree on a zoom-in with reasonable probability using their
advice. The same cannot be said for zoom-outs however, and to circumvent this issue we must develop
further tools. In this section, we define the notion of maximal zoom-outs and show that for a fixed zoom-in
Q, the number of maximal zoom-outs is bounded.

5.3.1     Generic Sets of Subspaces
To deal with large collections of zoom-outs we define a special property of zoom-outs that is called “gener-
icness”. To motivate it, let Wamb be an ambient space and suppose W1 , W2 ⊆ Wamb are distinct subspaces
of codimension r. Then W1 ∩ W2 is a subspace whose codimension is between 2r and r + 1. For a typical
pair of subspaces, the intersection W1 ∩ W2 has codimension 2r though, and in this case we say the two
subspaces are generic. Genericness is useful probabilistically because if W1 , W2 are generic, then the event
that a randomly chosen 2ℓ-dimensional subspace is contained in W1 , and the event the subspace is contained

                                                                           33
in W2 , are almost independent. Below is a formal definition which also applies to a set of subspaces rather
than just two:
Definition 5.11. We say that a set S = {W1 , . . . , WN } of codimension r subspaces of Wamb   T is t-generic
with respect to Wamb if for any t distinct subspaces, say Wi1 , . . . , Wit ∈ S, we have codim( 1⩽j⩽t Wij ) =
t · r. When the ambient space Wamb is clear from context we simply say that S is t-generic.
     Throughout this section, it will be convenient to denote by codimV (W ) the codimension of W ∩ Wamb
with respect to Wamb . When no subscript is used, the identity of the subspace Wamb should be clear from
context.
     We remark that any set of subspaces that is t-generic with respect to Wamb is also t′ -generic with respect
to Wamb for any t′ < t. In this section we show a sunflower-type lemma, stating that any large set of
codimension r subspaces inside Wamb contains a large set of subspaces that are t-generic with respect to
   ′
Wamb               ′
       for some Wamb   ⊆ Wamb . Below is a formal statement.
Lemma 5.12. Let t, r ∈ N be integers and let S be a set of N subspaces each with codimension r inside of
                                     ′
Wamb . Then there exists a subspace Wamb ⊆ Wamb and a set of subspaces S ′ ⊆ S such that:
                         1
                   (r+1)·(t−1)!
    • |S ′ | ⩾ N      q 3r
                                  .

    • Each Wi ∈ S ′ is contained in Wamb
                                     ′                                          ′ , where s ⩽ r.
                                         and has codimension s with respect to Wamb
    • S ′ is t-generic with respect to Wamb
                                        ′ .

    To show Lemma 5.12 we need two auxiliary lemmas. The first, Lemma 5.13, states that for j ⩾ 2, any
j-generic set of subspaces contains a large (j + 1)-generic set of subspaces. The second, Lemma 5.14, states
that a given collection of subspaces is either already 2-generic, or else many subspaces in the collection are
contained in a common hyperplane.
Lemma 5.13. Let S = {W1 , . . . , WN } be a set of N -subspaces of codimension r inside of Wamb that is
                                                                                                          1/j
j-generic with respect to Wamb for j ⩾ 2, then there is a subset {W1 , . . . , WN ′ } ⊆ S of size N ′ ⩾ Nqr
that is (j + 1)-generic with respect to Wamb .
Proof. Fix any j distinct subspaces in S, say W1 , . . . , Wj and let W = W1 ∩ · · · ∩ Wj . Since S is j-generic,
codim(W ) = j · r. We claim that there are at most q j·r subspaces Wi′ ∈ S \ {W1 , . . . , Wj } such that
codim(W ∩ Wi′ ) ⩽ (j + 1)r − 1. Call such subspaces bad, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that
there are more than q jr bad subspaces Wi′ . Then for each bad Wi′ we have,
       dim(Wi′ + W ) = dim(Wi′ ) + dim(W ) − dim(Wi′ ∩ W )
                             ⩽ (dim(Wamb ) − r) + (dim(Wamb ) − jr) − (dim(Wamb ) − (j + 1)r + 1)
                             = dim(Wamb ) − 1.
Therefore, for each Wi′ , the space W + Wi′ is contained in a hyperplane H such that H ⊇ W . There are
at most q codim(W ) − 1 = q j·r − 1 hyperplanes H containing W , and by the pigeonhole principle it follows
that there are two bad subspaces say Wi′1 , Wi′2 that are both contained in the same hyperplane H. This is a
contradiction however, as by the j-genericness of S, we must have
                          dim(Wi′1 + Wi′2 ) = dim(Wi′1 ) + dim(Wi′2 ) − dim(Wi′1 ∩ Wi′2 )
                                           = 2(dim(Wamb ) − r) − (dim(Wamb ) − 2r)
                                           = dim(Wamb ),


                                                        34
and hence Wi′1 and Wi′2 cannot both be contained in the hyperplane H.
   The lemma now follows from the claim we have just shown. Construct a subset S ′ greedily as follows:

   1. Initialize S ′ by picking j arbitrary subspaces from S and inserting them to S ′ .

   2. For any j subspaces in S ′ , say W1 , . . . , Wj , remove any W ′ ∈ S which is bad for them.

   3. If S is not empty, pick some W ∈ S, insert it to S ′ and iterate.

Note that trivially, the collection S ′ will be (j + 1)-generic in the end of the process. To lower bound
the size of S ′ , note that when |S ′ | = s, the number of elements from S that have been deleted is at most
sj q jr , and hence so long as this value is less than N , we may do another iteration. Thus, we must have that
       1/j          1/j
s ⩾ qNjr         = Nqr when the process terminates.

m ⩾ 1, at least one of the following holds.

    • There are m subspaces, say W1 , . . . , Wm that are 2-generic with respect to Wamb .
                           ′

      W1 , . . . , W N ′ .

W1 , . . . , WN and Wi , Wj are adjacent if and only if i ̸= j and codim(Wi ∩ Wj ) ⩽ 2r − 1. If every vertex
in this graph has degree at most ⌊ N    m ⌋, then we are done as there is an independent set of size m and these
subspaces satisfy the first condition. Suppose this is not the case. Then there is a vertex, say WN , that has

    In addition to Lemma 5.12, we state another useful feature of generic sets of subspaces, formalized
in Lemma 5.16 below. The lemma asserts that if a collection {W1 , . . . , WN } is generic, and one zooms-outs
from the ambient space Wamb into a hyperplane H, then one gets an induced collection {W1 ∩H, . . . , WN ∩
H} which is almost as generic.

Lemma 5.16. Let S = {W1 , . . . , WN } be a collection of subspaces of codimension r that is t-generic with
respect to some space Wamb for an even integer t, and let H be a hyperplane in Wamb . Then the collection
of subspaces S ′ = {W1 ∩ H, . . . , WN ∩ H} can be made a 2t -generic set of subspaces with respect to H
with codimension r inside of H by removing at most 2t subspaces Wi ∩ H from it.

Proof. Suppose that S ′ is not 2t -generic with respect to H with codimension r inside of H, as otherwise we
are done. In this case, there must exist 2t distinct subspaces, say W1 ∩ H, . . . , W t ∩ H ∈ S ′ such that the
                                                                                      2
codimension of W1 ∩ · · · ∩ W t ∩ H with respect to H, is less than tr/2. As H has codimension 1 with
                                  2
respect to Wamb it follows that
                                                                    t
                                    codim W1 ∩ · · · ∩ W t ∩ H ⩽ · r,                                     (12)
                                                            2         2
where now the codimension is with respect to Wamb . Since S is t-generic (and thus 2t -generic as well) with
respect to Wamb , this implies that W1 ∩ · · · ∩ W t ⊆ H. Indeed, this is because W1 ∩ · · · ∩ W t already has
                                                           2                                        2
codimension tr2 , so by (12), further intersecting with H does not result in a smaller subspace, and hence this
subspace must be inside H.
   Now delete W1 ∩ H, . . . , W t ∩ H from S ′ . We claim that the resulting set is 2t -generic with respect to
                                   2
H. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that it is not. Then there must be another 2t distinct subspaces, say
W t +1 ∩ H, . . . , Wt ∩ H ∈ S ′ such that W t +1 ∩ · · · ∩ Wt ⊆ H, implying
  2                                            2

   The following result is a version of Lemma 5.16 for codimensions larger than 1:

Lemma 5.17. Let W be a collection of subspaces of codimension r that is 2K -generic with respect to Wamb
and let B be a subspace of codimension j. Then, there is a set of subspaces WB such that the following
holds

    • Each WB ∈ WB is equal to W ∩ B for some W ∈ W.

    • The set of subspaces WB is 2K−j -generic with respect to B and each subspace in WB has codimen-
      sion r.

    • |W| − 2K ⩽ |WB | ⩽ |W|

Proof. We will obtain WB by removing a small number of subspaces from {W ∩ B | W ∈ W}. There is
a sequence of subspaces Wamb = B0 ⊇ B1 ⊇ · · · ⊇ Bj = B, such that Bi+1 is a hyperplane inside of Bi .
Do the following,

   1. Initialize W0 = W and set i = 1.

   2. Set Wi = {W ∩Bi | W ∈ Wi−1 }. Then, using Lemma 5.16, remove at most 2K−i−1 -many subspaces
      to turn Wi into a 2K−i -generic collection with respect to Bi .

   3. Stop if i = j, otherwise, increase i by 1 and return to step 2.

    We verify the three properties. The first property holds by construction. For the second property, it
is clear that the output is a set of subspaces Wj ⊆ WB that is 2K−j -generic with respect to B, and by
Lemma 5.16, the codimension of each W ∩ B ∈ WB with respect to B is still r. Finally, for the third prop-
erty, note that during
                     Peach  iteration, at most 2K−i−1 subspaces are removed by Lemma 5.16, so altogether
                       K−1 i
we remove at most i=0 2 ⩽ 2K subspaces.

5.3.2   The Sampling Lemma
As explained earlier, the notion of genericness is useful probabilistically, and in this section we state and
prove a sampling lemma about generic collections which is necessary for our analysis. We work inside a
subspace Wamb . Fix an arbitrary zoom-in Q ⊆ Wamb of dimension a, and let S = {W1 , . . . , Wm } be
a 2-generic collection of subspaces of Wamb of codimension r all containing Q. Also let A be a set of j-
dimensional subspaces containing Q. Consider the following two probability measures over Zoomj [Q, Wamb ]:

   1. The distribution µ which is uniform over Zoomj [Q, Wamb ].


                                                      37
     2. The distribution νS , wherein a subspace is sampled by first picking i ∈ {1, . . . , m} uniformly and
        then sampling a subspace from Zoomj [Q, Wi ] uniformly.

Throughout this section one should think of j as much larger than r and a, and dim(Wamb ) as much larger
than j. Concretely, we require j ⩾ 2100 r2 a2 and dim(Wamb ) ⩾ 2100 j. The main content of this section is
the following lemma, asserting that the measures µ and νS are close in statistical distance provided that m
is large. More precisely:
                                                                                                                    2 2
Lemma 5.18. Let Wamb be the ambient space and let a, r, j be dimensions such that j ⩾ 2100r a ,
dim(Wamb ) ⩾ 2100j . Let Q ⊆ Wamb be a subspace of dimension dim(Q) = a, and let S be a 2-generic col-
lection of subspaces of Wamb such that |S| = m, each subspace in S has codimension r with respect Wamb ,
and each subspace in S contains Q. Then, for any set of j-dimensional subspaces L ⊂ Zoomj [Q, Wamb ],
we have                                                     r

5.3.3   An Upper Bound on the Number of Maximal Zoom-outs
For this subsection, we work in the second prover’s space, FVq , and make the assumption that |V | ≫ ℓ, say
|V | ⩾ 2100 q ℓ to be concrete. We first establish several results in the simplified setting where there is no
zoom-in. After that we show how to deduce an analogous result with a zoom-in. Throughout this section,
we fix T to be a table that assigns, to each L ∈ Grass(FVq , 2ℓ), a linear function on L.
Definition 5.22. Given a table T on Grass(FVq , 2ℓ) and a subspace Q ⊆ FVq , we call a zoom-out, function
pair, (W, gW ), where Q ⊆ W ⊆ FVq and gW : W →    − Fq , (C, ζ)-maximal with respect to T on Q if
                                   Pr          [gW |L ≡ T [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ] ⩾ C,
                             L∈Grass(FV
                                      q ,2ℓ)


and there does not exist another zoom-out function pair, (W ′ , gW ′ ) such that FVq ⊇ W ′ ⊋ W , gW ′ : W ′ →
                                                                                                            −
Fq , gW ′ |W ≡ g|W and
                                 Pr           [gW ′ |L ≡ T [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ′ ] ⩾ ζC.
                            L∈Grass(FV
                                     q ,2ℓ)


In the case that Q = {0}, we say that (W, gW ) is (C, ζ)-maximal with respect to T .

                                                         40
    In the above statement, C should be thought of as small and ζ ∈ (0, 1] should be thought of as an
absolute constant. With this in mind, one should think of a zoom-out function pair (W, gW ) as being (C, ζ)
maximal if the agreement between gW and the table T on entries in Zoom2ℓ [Q, W ] is not “explained” by the
agreement of a larger zoom-out function pair, (W ′ , gW ′ ), where W ′ ⊇ W and gW ′ |W = gW . By definition
every zoom-out function pair (W, f ), where f has non-trivial agreement with T inside W , is in some sense
contained in a maximal zoom-out function pair (W ′ , f ′ ) (possibly with slightly lower agreement). This
point is encapsulated by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.23. Let T be a table on Grass(FVq , 2ℓ), Q ⊆ FVq , and W ⊆ FVq be a subspace of codimension r
containing Q. Suppose that there exists a linear function gW : W →
                                                                 − Fq such that

                                    Pr         [gW |L ≡ T [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ] ⩾ C.
                              L∈Grass(FV
                                       q ,2ℓ)


Then there exists a subspace W ′ ⊇ W and a linear function gW ′ : W ′ →
                                                                      − Fq such that gW ′ |W ≡ gW and
(gW ′ , W ′ ) is (Cζ −r , ζ)-maximal.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Definition 5.22. If (W, gW ) is (C, ζ)-maximal then we are
done. Otherwise, there must exist W1 , gW1 such that W1 ⊋ W , gW1 |W ≡ gW and

                                  Pr          [gW1 |L ≡ T [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W1 ] ⩾ ζC.
                             L∈Grass(FV
                                      q ,2ℓ)


We can repeat this argument at most r times before obtaining some (gW ′ , W ′ ) that is (Cζ −r , ζ) maximal
and satisfies W ′ ⊇ W and gW ′ |W ≡ gW .

    Next, we will want to upper bound the number of maximal zoom-outs. The following lemma (which is
a formal version of Lemma 1.7 from the introduction) is the key component in this upper bound. We set the
following parameters for the remainder of the section, which can all be considered constant:

6       Analysis of the PCP
In this section we analyze the PCP construction Ψ from Section 4. As usual, the completeness analysis is
straightforward and the soundness analysis will comprise the bulk of our effort.

6.1     Completeness
Suppose that the 3Lin instance (X, Eq) has an assignment σ : X →          − Fq that satisfies at least 1 − ε1 of
the equations in Eq. Let Usat ⊆ U be the set of all U = (e1 , . . . , ek ) where all k equations e1 , . . . , ek are
satisfied. Then, |Usat | ⩾ (1 − kε1 )|U|. We identify σ with the linear function from FX  q → Fq , assigning the
value σ(i) to the ith elementary basis element ei . Abusing notation, we denote this linear map by σ as well.
     For each U ∈ Usat and vertex L⊕HU , we set T1 [L⊕HU ] ≡ σ|L⊕HU . Since U ∈ Usat , these assignments
satisfy the side conditions. For all other U ’s, set T1 [L ⊕ HU ] so that the side conditions of HU are satisfied
and T1 [L ⊕ HU ]|L ≡ σ|L . Such an assignment is possible because L ∩ HU = {0}. Similarly, the table T2
is defined as T2 [R] ≡ σ|R .
     Sampling a constraint, note that the constraint is satisfied whenever the L′ ⊕ HU ′ chosen in step 3 of the
test satisfies that U ′ ∈ Usat . As the marginal distribution of L′ ⊕ HU ′ is uniform,9 the distribution of U ′ is
uniform. It follows that the constraint is satisfied whenever U ′ ∈ Usat , which happens with probability at
least 1 − kε1 . Thus, val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − kε1 .
    9
    This is true because first a clique is chosen with probability that is proportional to its size and then a vertex is sampled uniformly
from the clique.


                                                                    43
6.2     Soundness
In this section we relate the soundness of the composed PCP to that of the outer PCP. More precisely, we
show:
Lemma 6.1. For all δ > 0 there are r ∈ N and ζ(δ) > 0 such that the following holds for sufficiently large
ℓ ∈ N, sufficiently small c ∈ (0, 1), and
                                     k = q 2(1+c)ℓ ,   β = q −2(1+2c/3)ℓ ,
as in (7).
    Let G⊗kβ,r be the parallel repetition of the Smooth Variable versus Equation Game with advice described
in Section 3.1.4, and let Ψ be the composed PCP described in Section 4 using the sufficiently large ℓ above.
If val(G⊗k         −ζ(δ)·ℓ2 , then val(Ψ) ⩽ 64q −2(1−1000δ)ℓ .
         β,r ) < q
    The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Lemma 6.1, and it heavily relies on the tools from Sec-
tion 5. We will show the contrapositive of Lemma 6.1. That is, we will show that if there are tables T1 and
T2 that are ε-consistent for ε > 64q −2ℓ(1−1000δ) , then this implies strategies for the two provers that have
                                        2
success probability at least q −ζ(δ)·O(ℓ ) .

6.2.1   Clique Consistency
To start, we will reduce to the case where T1 satisfies a condition called clique-consistency.
Definition 6.2. We say an assignment T to A is clique consistent if for every vertex L1 ⊕ HU1 and for every
L2 ⊕ HU2 , L3 ⊕ HU3 ∈ [L1 ⊕ HU1 ], the assignments T [L2 ⊕ HU2 ] and T [L3 ⊕ HU3 ] satisfy the 1-to-1
constraint between L2 ⊕ HU2 and L3 ⊕ HU3 specified in Lemma 4.2.
   The following lemma shows that if T1 and T2 are ε-consistent assignments to Ψ, then there is a clique-
consistent assignment T1′ such that T1′ and T2 are ε-consistent.
Lemma 6.3. Suppose that the assignments T1 and T2 are ε-consistent, then there is a clique-consistent
assignment T1′ such that T1′ and T2 are ε-consistent.
Proof. Partition A into cliques, A = Clique1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Cliquem . For each i, choose a random L ⊕ HU ∈
Cliquei uniformly, and for every L′ ⊕ HU ′ ∈ Cliquei assign T1′ [L′ ⊕ HU ′ ] in the unique way that is
consistent with T1 [L ⊕ HU ] and the side conditions of U ′ as described in Lemma 4.2. It is clear that T1′ is
clique consistent, and we next analyze the expected fraction of constraints that T1′ and T2 satisfy.
     Note that an alternative description of sampling a constraint in Ψ proceeds as follows. First choose a
clique Cliquei with probability that is proportional to by its size, and then choose L ⊕ HU ∈ Cliquei in the
first step. The rest of the sampling procedure is the same. Let P (L ⊕ HU ) be the probability that the test
passes conditioned on L ⊕ HU being chosen in the second step. It is clear that every vertex in the clique has
equal probability of being chosen, therefore the probability of passing if Cliquei is chosen is
                                         1         X
                                                             P (L ⊕ HU ).
                                     |Cliquei |
                                               L⊕HU ∈Cliquei

On the other hand, the expected fraction of constraints satisfied by T1′ and T2 (over the randomness of
choosing T1′ ) is
                  X           1                           1          X
                                     · P (L ⊕ HU ) =                         P (L ⊕ HU ).
                          |Cliquei |                  |Cliquei |
               L⊕HU ∈Cliquei                                        L⊕HU ∈Cliquei


                                                       44
To see this, note that for any L ⊕ HU , |Clique 1
                                                     is the probability that T1 [L ⊕ HU ] is used to define T1′ on
                                                  i|
Cliquei . If this is the case, then the probability the test passes on T1′ within Cliquei is P (L ⊕ HU ).
    Since this holds over every clique, it follows that the expected fraction of constraints satisfied by T1′
equals the fraction of constraints satisfied by T1 and T2 . In particular, there is a choice of T1′ such that
together with T2 it satisfies at least ε fraction of the constraints.

    Applying Lemma 6.3 we conclude that there are clique-consistent assignments to Ψ that are ε-consistent,
and henceforth we assume that T1 is clique-consistent to begin with. We remark that, in the notation of Sec-
tion 4.1.4, the benefit of having a clique-consistent assignment is that the constraint that the verifier checks
is equivalent to checking that T1 [L ⊕ HU ]|R ≡ T2 [R]. The latter check is a test which is performed within
the space FU q of the first prover. We will use this fact in the next section.


6.2.2   A Strategy for the First Prover

EU [p(U )] ⩾ ε. By an averaging argument, p(U ) ⩾ 2ε for at least 2ε -fraction of the U ’s. Call such U ’s good
and let Ugood be the set of good U ’s.

gives up, so henceforth assume that U ∈ Ugood . For such U , the test of the inner PCP passes with probability
at least 2ε . More concretely, we have

                                                                  46
6.2.3   A Strategy for the Second Prover
Let V be the question to the second prover. The second prover will use a table T1′ to derive their strategy.
The table T1′ is obtained from T1 as follows. For a question V to the second prover, let U ′ ⊇ V be an
arbitrary question to the first prover. For all 2ℓ-dimensional subspaces L ⊆ FVq , define

                                            T1′ [L] ≡ T1 [L ⊕ HU ′ ]|L .

If L ∩ HU ′ ̸= {0}, then we arbitrarily define T ′ [L] to be the 0 function. Note that provided |V | ⩾ 2k, this
will only be the case for a negligible, q 2ℓ−k , fraction of subspaces L.
Using clique consistency: we first argue that T1′ is well defined, and for that we make several observations.
                                                                      ′                               U′
First, recall that the subspace L ⊕ HU ′ is indeed a subspace of FU                             V
                                                                     q because we have L ⊆ Fq ⊆ Fq , where
                                         ′                                           ′
FVq is viewed as the subspace of FU    q where the restriction to coordinates in U \ V is 0. Next, note that
                   ′                                     ′
all choices of U ⊇ V lead to the same value of T1 [L]. Indeed, for a fixed L, the vertices L ⊕ HU ′ over
all U ′ ⊇ V are in the same clique, so since T1 is clique consistent they all receive a consistent value with
regards to the mapping in Lemma 4.2 and hence lead to the same function T1 [L ⊕ HU ′ ]|L . Therefore the
second prover can construct the table T1′ , and we can assume without loss of generality that the U ′ that was
chosen is the same question that the first prover received, i.e. U ′ = U .
     After constructing T1′ , the second prover then chooses a dimension 0 ⩽ r′ ⩽ 10       δ uniformly for the
advice Q. That is, the set Q to be the span of the first r′ advice vectors that they receive. Note that with
                         1
probability at least 10/δ+1    the second prover also chooses r′ = r1 (that is, they choose the same dimension
as the first prover for the advice). The second prover then uniformly chooses a zoom-out function pair
(Wsecond , gQ,Wsecond ) that is                             
                                                   C       1
                                                        ,      -maximal
                                               4 · 510/δ 5
with respect to T1′ on Q (the second prover gives up if such pair does not exist).
     Finally, the second prover extends the function gQ,Wsecond randomly to a linear function on FVq to arrive
at their answer. The resulting function is linear and it is equal to the inner product function y →
                                                                                                  − ⟨sQ,V , y⟩
for some unique string sQ,V ∈ FVq . The second prover outputs sQ,V as their answer.

6.2.4   The Success Probability of the Provers
In order to be successful, a series of events must occur. We go through each one and state the probability
that each occurs. At the end this yields a lower bound on the provers’ success probability. We remark that
the analysis of this sections requires Lemmas 5.5 and 5.7, so recall that k and β are set according to (7)
in Section 5.2 so that these lemmas hold.
    First, the provers need U ∈ Ugood , which occurs with probability at least 2ε . Assuming that this occurs,
the provers then both need to choose r′ = r1 for the dimension of their zoom-in, which happens with
                      δ2
probability at least 101 . If both provers do choose r′ = r1 , then both prover’s set Q to be the span of the first
r1 advice vectors that they receive and in particular, receive the same zoom-in Q as advice. Going forward,
we will only analyze the provers’ success probability assuming that this is the case.
    The provers then need Q ∈ Qlucky , Q ∩ HU = {0}, and Q ∈ Qsmooth . When analyzing the probability
that these three events occur, we need to recall that the advice vectors are actually drawn uniformly from the
second prover’s space FVq , rather than FU  q . To this end, let us define the following coupled distribution.




                                                        47
The Advice Distribution, Dadv :

    • Choose V ⊆ U according to the outer PCP.

    • Choose x1 , . . . , xr1 ∈ FVq uniformly.

    • Choose w1 , . . . , wr1 ∈ HU uniformly.

    • Set Q = span(x1 , . . . , xr1 ) and Q′ = span(x1 + w1 , . . . , xr1 + wr1 ).
                                                                                                       1 ,
     Thus, the subspace Q is actually drawn according to the first marginal of Dadv , which we denote Dadv
while the second marginal of Dadv is the distribution Dr′ 1 from Section 5.2.3.
     The following claim will help us move from statements about Q′ to statements about Q. It is help-
ful, because the covering property from Section 5.2.3 will allow us to bound probabilities about Q′ , while
Claim 6.4 will allow us to translate these into statements about Q.

Claim 6.4. Let (Q, Q′ ) be output by Dadv . Then the following two statements are true.

    • If Q′ ∈ Qlucky , Q′ ∩ HU = {0}, then Q ∈ Qlucky , Q ∩ HU = {0}.

follows from (18). It is also easy to see that Q ∩ HU = {0}.

the same. The fourth transition follows the same reasoning as the second transition, and the final transition
is by definition of L′Q .

7      Proofs of the Main Theorems
7.1     Proof of Theorem 1.3
Theorem 1.3 follows by applying our PCP construction from Section 4 starting with an instance of Gap3Lin
from Theorem 2.6. Fix any τ, ε, which will parameterize our target completeness and soundness in Theo-
rem 1.3 and let s be the value from Theorem 2.6.
   We apply the construction of Section 4 with parameters as follows. Set q = 2, set some sufficiently
small δ > 0 with respect to ε, set r = ⌈10/δ⌉, set ℓ sufficiently large relative to δ −1 and s, set c sufficiently
small relative to δ, and set
                                     k = q 2(1+c)ℓ and q −2(1+2c/3)ℓ .
Apply this construction to a Gap3Lin[1 − η, s] where the completeness value is set with η ⩽ τ /k. The
completeness and soundness of the final PCP are as follows.
   If the original 3Lin instance is at least 1 − η satisfiable, our final PCP satisfies val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − kη ⩾ 1 − τ .
This gives the desired completeness.
   On the other hand, if the original instance is at most s satisfiable for some constant s > 0, then
by Claim 3.2, the value of the outer PCP is at most
                                                             2c 
                                               −Ω (1−s)2 q −r+ 3 ℓ                2
                                val(G⊗k
                                     β,r ) ⩽ 2                       < q −ζ(δ)O(ℓ) ,

since we take ℓ sufficiently large compared to δ −1 . By Lemma 6.1 it follows that if the original instance is
at most s satisfiable, then val(Ψ) ⩽ 64q −2(1−1000δ)ℓ . The proof is concluded as the alphabet size of Ψ is
O(q 2ℓ ).

7.2     Proof of Theorem 1.4
To show quasi-NP-hardness for approximate Quadratic Programming, we rely on the following result due
to [ABH+ 05], who show a reduction from 2-Prover-1-Round Games to Quadratic Programming.

Theorem 7.1. There is a reduction from a 2-Prover-1-Round Games, Ψ with graph G = (L ∪ R, E) and
alphabets ΣL , ΣR to a Quadratic Programming instance A such that:

      • The running time of the reduction and the number of variables in A are both polynomial in |L| + |R|
        and 2|ΣL | .
                                                    1
      • If val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − η, then OPT(A) ⩾ 1 − η − |L|+|R| .

                                                       52
    • If val(Ψ) ⩽ ε, then OPT(A) ⩽ O(ε).

   We are now ready to prove Theorem 1.4.

Proof of Theorem 1.4. Starting with a SAT instance of size n, we take the instance of Gap3Lin from Theo-
                            2
rem 2.7 of size N ⩽ 2O(log n) with field size q = 2 as the starting point of our reduction. Take δ > 0 to be
a small constant, r = ⌈10/δ⌉, k = (log n)C for a large constant C and pick c sufficiently small relative to δ
and ℓ and β correspondingly so that (7) holds.
                          2
    This yields a 2O(k log n) -time reduction from SAT to a 2-Prover-1-Game on G = (L ∪ R, E), with
alphabets ΣL , ΣR and the following properties:

    • |R| + |L| = O(N k · q 3k+2ℓ ).

    • |ΣR | ⩽ |ΣL | = q 2ℓ = k 1/(1+c) .
                                                                √
    • The completeness is at least 1 − kη, where η = 2−Θ( log n) .

    • The soundness is at most q −2(1−1000δ)ℓ .

Indeed, the first 3 properties are clear. For the soundness, as the original 3Lin instance is at most 1 − ε
satisfiable for ε = Ω(1/ log3 N ), we get from Claim 3.2 that
                                                                 
                                                              2cℓ
                                                −Ω ε−2 q −r+ 3                   2
                               val G⊗k
                                    β,r ⩽ 2                           ⩽ q −ζ(δ)O(ℓ) .

Then the soundness of the composed PCP is at most q −2(1−1000δ)ℓ by Lemma 6.1. Applying the reduction
of Theorem 7.1, we get a reduction from SAT to a Quadratic Programming instance A such that,

    • The running time of the reduction and number of variables in A are both polynomial in
                                              2   2(1+c)ℓ            2(1+c)ℓ ) q 2ℓ
                                                                                     
                              M = poly 2O(log n)q          (log n)O(q          2       .

    • If the original SAT instance is satisfiable, then
                                                                    √
                                                OPT ⩾ 1 − 2−Ω( log n) .

    • If the original SAT instance is not satisfiable, then
                                                                 
                                            OPT ⩽ O q −2(1−1000δ)ℓ .

Note that
                                           log(M ) = q 2(1+c)ℓ O log2 n ,
                                                                       

whereas the gap between the satisfiable and unsatisfiable cases is Ω q −2(1−1000δ)ℓ = log(M )11−O(δ) . Alto-
                                                                                   
                                                                                            C
                                                                                               
gether, this shows that for all ε > 0 there is C > 0 such that unless NP ⊆ DTIME 2     log(n)    , there is no
log(M )1−ε -approximation algorithm for Quadratic Programming on M variables.




                                                          53
7.3     Proof of Theorem 1.5
In this section we prove Theorem 1.5, and for that we must first establish a version of Theorem 1.3 for
biregular graphs of bounded degree. The proof of this requires minor modifications of our construction, as
well as the right degree reduction technique of Moshkovitz and Raz [MR10].

7.3.1    Obtaining a Hard Instance of Bipartite Biregular 2-CSP
We first show that the 2-Prover-1-Round game from Theorem 1.3 can be transformed into a hard instance of
biregular, bipartite 2-CSP with bounded degrees. This version may be useful for future applications, and is
formally stated below. Call a bipartite 2-CSP (d1 , d2 )-regular if the left degrees of its underlying graph are
all d1 , and the right degrees of its underlying graph are all d2 . Throughout this section, for a 2-CSP, Ψ, and
an assignment to it, F , we will use val(F ) to denote the fraction of constraints in Ψ that F satisfies.

Theorem 7.2. For every φ, ε > 0, and sufficiently large R ∈ N, there exist d1 , d2 ∈ N such that given
a bipartite (d1 , d2 )-regular 2-CSP, Ψ, with alphabet size R, it is NP-hard to distinguish the following two
cases:

      • Completeness: val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − φ,
                             1
      • Soundness: val(Ψ) ⩽ R1−ε .

    To prove Theorem 7.2, we start with an instance Ψ from Theorem 1.3 and show how to modify it to be
biregular, while mostly preserving its value. We will do so in two steps, first making Ψ left-regular, and then
applying a generic expander-based transformation (similar to that of Moshkovitz and Raz [MR10]) to gain
bounded-degree right-regularity.
    Fix φ, ε > 0, and let Ψ be the 2-Prover-1-Round game constructed for Theorem 1.3. Recall that this
requires us to choose some large enough ℓ relative to φ−1 , ε−1 , some large enough q relative to ℓ, and set
                              ε
R = q 2ℓ . We also set δ = 1000 , 0 < c arbitrarily small relative to δ, and k = q 2(1+c)ℓ , q = 2. Finally, we
construct our 2-Prover-1-Round game from a hard instance of Gap3Lin with the appropriate completeness
and soundness, so that it is NP-hard to distinguish between,
                                                                    1               1
                          val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − φ and        val(Ψ) ⩽                   =          .
                                                               q 2(1−1000δ)ℓ       R1−ε

   Claim 7.4 allows us to remove trivial vertices without affecting the value much. Towards going from a
weighted 2-CSP to an unweighted 2-CSP, we will next bound the size of the neighborhoods in Ψ′ .

Claim 7.5. For each non-trivial L ⊕ HU we have |nb(L ⊕ HU )| ⩽ 10k q 6kℓ .

Proof. Let U = (x1 , . . . , x3k ) = (e1 , . . . , ek ) and suppose equation ei contains variables (x3i−2 , x3i−1 , x3i ).
Since L ⊕ HU is not trivial, for each i, there must be a point v ∈ L ⊆ FU        q such that the values of v restricted
to the coordinates of variables (x3i−2 , x3i−1 , x3i ) are not all equal. Without loss of generality, say that it is
x3i for each 1 ⩽ i ⩽ k. It follows that in order to have

                                        L ⊕ HU ⊕ HU ′ = L′ ⊕ HU ⊕ HU ′ ,

U ′ must contain an equation with the variable x3i for each 1 ⩽ i ⩽ k. Let Ei denote this set of equations
for each i. By the regularity assumptions on our 3Lin instance, |Ei | ⩽ 10 and Ei ∩ Ej = ∅ for i ̸= j. It
follows that U ′ must contain exactly one equation from each Ei , and that these form all k equations of U ′ ,
so there are at most 10k possible U ′ for which there can exist L′ ⊆ U ′ , such that L′ ⊕ HU ′ ∈ [L ⊕ HU ]. The
lemma follows from the observation that |Grassq (3k, 2(1 − δ)ℓ)| ⩽ q 6kℓ .

    Performing the same procedure as in [KR03, Lemma 3.4], we can turn Ψ′ into a bipartite, left-regular
2-CSP instance, while again not affecting its value too much. Let Q = 10k q 6kℓ be the upper bound on
neighborhood sizes in Claim 7.5.

bipartite expander graph, with N vertices on each side, vertex degree D, and second eigenvalue O(D1/2 ).
When N is sufficiently large relative to D, the algorithm is from [Alo21], and otherwise, a brute force
algorithm suffices.

     We are now ready to complete the proof of Theorem 7.2.

Proof of Theorem 7.2. Fix φ, ε > 0 as in Theorem 7.2 and take Ψ to be the 2-CSP from Theorem 1.3 with
δ therein set to φ/2, ε therein set to ε/2, and set ℓ and therefore R sufficiently large, so that distinguishing
between val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − φ/2 and val(Ψ) ⩽ R−(1−ε/2) is NP-hard. Starting from Ψ, obtain Ψ′ by deleting
trivial vertices and applying the transformation in Claim 7.6 with C = q 10ℓ , and then let Ψ′′ be obtained
from Ψ′ via the transformation in Lemma 7.8 with D = q 10ℓ . It is clear that Ψ′′ is (C · Q · D, D)-regular,
has alphabet size R, and is constructed from Ψ in polynomial time.
     It remains to show that the transformations performed preserve the value. By Claim 7.4 and Claim 7.6,
we have
                   val(Ψ) − 2q −(2−2c)ℓ − q −10ℓ ⩽ val(Ψ′ ) ⩽ val(Ψ) + 3q −(2−2c)ℓ + q −10ℓ ,
and by Lemma 7.8, we have
                                                                         
                                 val(Ψ′ ) ⩽ val(Ψ′′ ) ⩽ val(Ψ′ ) + O q −5ℓ ,

                                                         58
so altogether we get that
                                                                                            
          val(Ψ) − 2q −(2−2c)ℓ − q −10ℓ ⩽ val(Ψ′′ ) ⩽ val(Ψ) + 3q −(2−2c)ℓ + q −10ℓ + O q −5ℓ .

Since we set ℓ and, as a result, R sufficiently large, it is clear that if val(Ψ) ⩾ 1−φ/2, then val(Ψ′′ ) ⩾ 1−φ,
and if val(Ψ) ⩽ R−(1−ε/2) then val(Ψ′′ ) ⩽ R−(1−ε) . This establishes Theorem 7.2.

7.3.2   Sparsification
In [LM24], Lee and Manurangsi show how to conclude Theorem 1.5 from Theorem 7.2 via a sparsification
procedure. We summarize the steps here. Fix the η > 0 for Theorem 1.5. Set φ = ε = 0.01η in Theorem 7.2
and let Ψ be the resulting hard bipartite (d1 , d2 )-regular 2-CSP, and it is NP-hard to distinguish between the
cases
                                                                            1
                                  val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − φ and val(Ψ) ⩽ 1−ε ,
                                                                         R
where R is the sufficiently large alphabet size. It is shown in [LM24, Lemma 10], stated below, that the
degrees of Ψ can be multiplied by arbitrary constants by simply copying vertices.

Lemma 7.9. [LM24, Lemma 10] For any integers d1 , d2 , c1 , c2 , there is a polynomial time reduction from
a bipartite (d1 , d2 )-biregular CSP, Ψ, to a bipartite (c2 d1 d2 , c1 d1 d2 )-biregular CSP Ψ′ , such that val(Ψ) =
val(Ψ′ ), and such that the left and right alphabet sizes are preserved.

    It is then shown in [LM24, Theorem 11], stated below, that one can perform a subsampling procedure
to Ψ′ that significantly lowers the degree.

Theorem 7.10. [LM24, Theorem 11] For any 0 < ν1 < ν2 ⩽ 1, any positive integer C, and any sufficiently
large positive integers dA , dB ⩾ d0 (φ, ν), and R ⩾ R0 (δ, ν, dA , dB ), the following holds: there is a
randomized polynomial-time reduction from a bipartite (dA C, dB C)-biregular 2-CSP, Ψ′ , with alphabet
size at most R, (dA , dB )-bounded degree 2-CSP, Ψ′′ , such that, with probability at least 2/3 the following
two items hold

    • Completeness: val(Ψ′′ ) ⩾ val(Ψ′ ) − ν1 ,
                                                                              
    • Soundness: If val(Ψ′ ) ⩽ R1ν2 , then val(Ψ′′ ) ⩽ ν2 −ν
                                                          1
                                                             1
                                                                      1    1
                                                                     dA + dB

    Putting everything together, we can prove Theorem 1.5.

Proof of Theorem 1.5. Recall the values η and d from Theorem 1.5. Start with an instance Ψ of 2-CSP
from Theorem 7.2 with φ = ε = 0.01η and sufficiently large alphabet size R. Then Ψ is (d1 , d2 )-biregular,
with sufficiently large alphabet size R relative to φ−1 , ε−1 , and d. For such a Ψ, it is NP-hard to distinguish
                                                  1
whether val(Ψ) = 1 − 0.01η, or val(Ψ) ⩽ R1−0.01η      .
     Applying Lemma 7.9 with c1 = c2 = d yields, in polynomial time, a (dd1 d2 , dd1 d2 )-biregular 2-CSP,
Ψ′ , with alphabet size R and satisfying val(Ψ′ ) = val(Ψ). Next, applying Theorem 7.10, with dA = d, dB =
d, C = d1 d2 , ν1 = 0.01η, ν2 = 1 − ε, we get (after randomized polynomial time reduction) a 2-CSP Ψ′′
with degree at most d such that:

    • If val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − φ, then val(Ψ′′ ) ⩾ 1 − φ − ν1 = 1 − 0.02η.
                    1

7.4     Proof of Theorem 1.6
Combining our 2-Prover-1-Round Game with the reductions in [Lae14] we obtain improved hardness of
approximation results for Rooted k-connectivity on undirected graphs, the vertex-connectivity survivable
network design problem, and the vertex-connectivity k-route cut problem on undirected graphs. We give an
overview of the reduction here. It will be more convenient to refer to 2-CSPs rather than 2-Prover-1-Round
Game for the remainder of this subsection.
    In the reduction of [Lae14] one uses a minimization variant for a 2-CSP Ψ, called Min-Rep. The
input in Min-Rep is still a 2-CSP, but now each alphabet symbol is assigned a cost, and a labeling of Ψ is
allowed to assign multiple alphabet symbols to each. The cost of a labeling is the total cost of the alphabet
symbols used over all vertices and MinRep(Ψ) is the minimum cost labeling such that every constraint of
Ψ is satisfied. Here, a constraint on the edge (u, v) is satisfied as long as one pair of labels from those
assigned to u and v respectively satisfy the constraint. We refer to [Lae14, page 1630] for a more formal
definition. The following lemma [Lae14] describes approximation preserving reductions from Min-Rep to
various connectivity problems.

Theorem 7.11. [Lae14, Theorem 3.1] There are polynomial-time, approximation-preserving reductions
which take an instance of minimum cost label cover with degree d and alphabet size R, and output:

      • an instance of rooted k-connectivity on undirected graphs with k = O(d3 · R + d4 ),

      • an instance of vertex-connectivity survivable network design on undirected graphs with maximum
        requirement k = O(d · R + d2 ),

      • an instance of vertex-connectivity k-route cut on undirected graphs with k = O(d · R)

   To obtain hardness of approximation results from Theorem 7.11, we must first obtain a hardness of
approximation result for Min-Rep. This can be done by using the following result due to [Man19].

Lemma 7.12. [Man19, Lemma 7] For any ε, γ > 0, there is a polynomial time randomized reduction
that, given any 2-CSP with projection constraints Ψ, alphabet size R, and size N , outputs a 2-CSP with
projection constraints Ψ′ with the same alphabet and maximum degree at most
                                                            
                                                 6 2 log(2R)
                                          d := 10      √
                                                         γ

such that

      • (Completeness) if val(Ψ) ⩾ 1 − ε, then MinRep(Ψ′ ) ⩽ (1 + ε · d)N with probability 0.9, and,
                                                            
      • (Soundness) if val(Π) < γ, then Min-Rep(Π′ ) > 0.06
                                                          √
                                                            γ N with probability 0.9.

      Using Lemma 7.12, we get the following hardness of approximation result for Min-Rep.

gree O(g log g) and alphabet size O(g 2+ε ), it is NP-hard under randomized reduction to approximate
the MinRep(Ψ) to within a factor of g.

1 + ε′ /2, and alphabet R such that the value in the no case is γ := R−(1−ε) = 2−11 g −2 . Let N denote

                                              0.03 · γ −1/2 ⩾ g.
                                                       ′
The alphabet size of Ψ′ is R = O(g 2/(1−ε) ) = O(g 2+ε ) and the maximum degree is at most d = O(g log g).


    Finally, by combining Theorem 7.11 with Theorem 7.13, we can show Theorem 1.6.

Proof of Theorem 1.6. Fix ε > 0 and g > 0 sufficiently large, and let Ψ be the 2-CSP from Theorem 7.13
such that MinRep(Ψ) is hard to approximate within factor g, and Ψ has maximum degree d = O(g log g)
and alphabet size R = O(g 2+ε ). By Theorem 7.11, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that produces
instances of the following problems which are hard to approximate within factor g:

    • rooted k-connectivity on undirected graphs with k = O(g 5+ε log g),

    • vertex-connectivity survivable network design on undirected graphs with maximum requirement k =
      O(g 3+ε log g),

    • vertex-connectivity k-route cut on undirected graphs with k = O(g 3+ε log(g)).

Expressing g in terms of k in each of the three cases gives the desired result.


8    Bounding the Number of Successful Zoom-outs of a Fixed Codimension
The goal of this section is to prove Lemma 5.24. Let us recall some context first. Throughout the section, we
work in the second prover’s space, FVq , where V is some question to the second prover (in the outer PCP).

n = |V |, we will write the ambient space as Fnq from now on. We fix T to be a table that assigns, to each

this section. Now, let us review the set up of Lemma 5.24. Recall that we set

                                                          63
8.3     Step 3: A Global Set with Local Agreement
The next step is to further refine the set L′X,σ so that the remaining subspaces “evenly cover” a subspace of
   ⋆
Wamb        ′
       ⊆ Wamb    with codimension dim(X) + Oδ2 (1). To do this, we will reduce to the case where L′X,σ
is pseudo-random within some zoom-in A and zoom-out B such that X ⊆ A ⊆ B. This is done via the
following argument, which can be informally described as:

   1. While L′X,σ is not pseudo-random, there must be some zoom-in or zoom-out on which its measure is
      significantly higher than its overall measure over the whole space, so consider the restriction to this
      zoom-in or zoom-out.

   2. This increases the measure of L′X,σ , and we may repeat until we have a pseudo-random set (within
      some zoom-in, zoom-out combination).

   3. By choosing the pseudo-randomness parameters suitably, we are able to perform the above process in
      relatively few times until the restriction of L′X,σ that we arrive at is pseudo-random.

   4. As a result, when restricting to the zoom-in, zoom-out combination, the resulting set of subspaces
                                                                                               −1
      evenly covers a space that is still relatively large in the sense that it contains q −O(ξ ) -fraction of
      the present space (i.e. the space obtained after iteratively restricting to zoom-ins and zoom-outs as
      described in steps 1-3). In particular, this present space is described as Wamb,0 below.

    Using Lemma 8.5 we conclude the proof of Lemma 5.24 by using ideas from the Raz-Safra analysis of
the Plane versus Plane test [RS97]. Define a graph, G with vertex set W ⋆ , where Wi⋆ , Wj⋆ are adjacent if
and only if fi⋆ |Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ = fj⋆ |Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ . We claim that this graph contains a large clique, and towards this end
we first show that the graph is nearly transitive. For a graph H = (V, E), define

                                   β(H) = max Pr [(v, u), (v, w) ∈ E].
                                                 / v∈V
                                            (u,w)∈E

A graph H is transitive if β(H) = 0. It is easy to see that transitive graphs are (edge) disjoint unions of
cliques. The following lemma, proved in [RS97], asserts that if H is relatively dense and β(H) is small,
then one could remove only a small fraction of the edges and get a fully transitive graph.

                                                          65
Lemma
  p   8.6. [RS97, Lemma 2] Any graph H on n vertices can be made transitive by deleting at most
⌈3 β(H) · n2 ⌉ edges.

      To use Lemma 8.6 we first show that the graph G we defined is highly transitive.

Claim 8.7. We have β(G) ⩽ m13 .

Proof. Fix a Wi⋆ , Wj⋆ that are not adjacent. We claim that they can have at most 1 common neighbor.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that Wa⋆ , Wb⋆ are distinct common neighbors. Then,

                  fi⋆ |Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ ∩Wa⋆ = fj⋆ |Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ ∩Wa⋆ ,         fi⋆ |Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ ∩Wb⋆ = fj⋆ |Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ ∩Wb⋆ .

It follows that fi⋆ and fj⋆ agree on Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wa⋆ + Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wb⋆ . However, since W ⋆ is 4-generic, we
have

      codim(Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wa⋆ + Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wb⋆ ) ⩽ 3s + 3s − 4s = 2s,                              codim(Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ) = 2s,

and
                               Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wa⋆ + Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wb⋆ ⊆ Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ,
so it must be the case that Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wa⋆ + Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Wb⋆ = Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ . This contradicts the assumption
that Wi⋆ and Wj⋆ are not adjacent. Thus, any two non-adjacent vertices can have at most 1 common neighbor,
and the result follows.

Claim 8.8. The graph G contains a clique of size at least m43

Proof. Applying Markov’s inequality to Lemma 8.5, we have that with probability at least 9/10 over Wi⋆
and Wj⋆ , we have both Prz∈Wi⋆ ∩Wj⋆ ∩Z [fi⋆ (z) ̸= fj⋆ (z)] ⩽ 10001γ and |Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Z| ⩾ 0.81 · |Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ |.
In this case, fi⋆ and fj⋆ agree on at least (1 − 10001γ)-fraction of the points in Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ ∩ Z, which is in
turn at least (1 − 10001γ) · 0.81 > 1/q-fraction of the points in Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ . As fi⋆ and fj⋆ are linear functions,
the Schwartz-Zippel lemma implies that such Wi⋆ , Wj⋆ are adjacent in G. Hence, G has at least 9 m23 /10
                                                                                                             

edges.

A     Proofs of Lemmas 2.3 and 2.4
In this section we prove Lemmas 2.3 and 2.4. The proofs of these lemmas requires tools from [EKL23,
EKL24] regarding Fourier analysis over the Bilinear Scheme.

A.1   Fourier Analysis over the Bilinear Scheme
The key to proving Lemma 2.3 is a level-d inequality for indicator functions on the Bilinear Scheme due
to Evra, Kindler, and Lifshitz [EKL24]. In order to use the result of [EKL24], however, we first give some
necessary background for Fourier analysis over the Bilinear Scheme, and describe the analogues of zoom-
ins, zoom-outs, and pseudo-randomness as in [EKL23, EKL24]. After doing so, we must then find a suitable
map from the Grassmann graph to the Bilinear Scheme that (1) preserves the edges of our original bipartite
inclusion graph between 2ℓ-dimensional and 2(1 − δ)ℓ subspaces, and (2) maps zoom-ins and zoom-outs in
the Grassmann graph to their analogues over the Bilinear Scheme.




                                                   71
The Bilinear Scheme: Let Fn×2ℓ  q    be the set of n×2ℓ matrices over Fq . One can define a graph over Fn×2ℓ
                                                                                                        q
that is similar to the Grassmann graphs by calling M1 , M2 ∈ Fqn×2ℓ adjacent if dim(ker(M1 − M2 )) ⩽ s
for some s ⩽ 2ℓ. Such graphs are often referred to as the Bilinear Scheme. For our purposes, we will need
                                                        (n−2ℓ)×2ℓ       (n−2(1−δ)ℓ)×2(1−δ)ℓ
to work with a bipartite version of this graph between Fq         and Fq                     .

    We now describe the analogues of zoom-ins and zoom-outs on Fn×2ℓ
                                                                   q    .

Definition A.1. A zoom-in of dimension d over Fn×2ℓ
                                                  q  is given by d-pairs of vectors (u1 , v1 ), . . . , (ur , vr )
where each ui ∈ F2ℓq  and  each v i ∈  F n . Let U ∈ F2ℓ×d and V ∈ Fn×d denote the matrices whose
                                         q             q                 q
ith columns are ui and vi respectively. Then the zoom-in on (U, V ) is the set of M ∈ Fn×2ℓ q        such that
M U = V , or equivalently, M ui = vi for 1 ⩽ i ⩽ d.

    Next, we define the analog of zoom-outs.

Definition A.2. A zoom-out of dimension d is defined similarly, except by multiplication on the left. Given
X ∈ Fd×nq   and Y ∈ Fd×2ℓ
                       q  , whose rows are given by xi and yi respectively, the zoom-out (X, Y ) is the
M ∈ Fq n×2ℓ such that XM = Y , or equivalently, xi M = yi for 1 ⩽ i ⩽ d.

   Let Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] denote the intersections of the zoom-in on (U, V ) and the zoom-out on
(X, Y ). The codimension of Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] is the sum of the number of columns of U and the
number of rows of X, which we will denote by dim(U ) and codim(X). For a zoom-in and zoom-out pair
and a Boolean function F , we define F(U,V ),(X,Y ) : Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] →
                                                                             − {0, 1} to be the restriction of
F which is given as

                        F(U,V ),(X,Y ) (M ) = F (M ) for M ∈ Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )].


                                                             72
When dim(U )+codim(X) = d, we say that the restriction is of size d. We define (d, ε)-pseudo-randomness
in terms of the L2 -norms of restrictions of F of size d. Here and throughout, when we consider restricted
functions, the underlying measure is the uniform measure over the corresponding zoom-in and zoom-out set
Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )].
Definition A.3. We say that an indicator function F ∈ L2 (Fn×2ℓ
                                                           q    ) is (d, ε)-pseudo-random if for all zoom-in
zoom-out combinations Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] such that dim(U ) + codim(X) = d, we have

    The following result is a combination of two results form [EKL24]. It states that if a Boolean function
F is (r, ε)-pseudo-random, then its degree d parts are (r, Cq,d ε2 )-pseudo-random for d ⩽ r.
Lemma A.5. [EKL24, Theorem 5.5 + Proposition 3.6] Let t ⩾ 4 be a power of 2 and let F : Fn×2ℓ    q     →
                                                            =d        10dr+500d2t 2
{0, 1} be a function that is (d, ε, t)-pseudo-random. Then F is (r, q            ε )-pseudo-random for all
r ⩾ d.
Proof. This lemma does not actually appear in [EKL24], but it is easy to derive by combining Theorem 5.5
with Proposition 3.6 therein. In [EKL23, EKL24], the authors introduce an additional notion of generalized
influences and having small generalized influences. We refrain from defining these notions explicitly as it is
slightly cumbersome, but roughly speaking, one defines a Laplacian for each zoom-in, zoom-out combina-
tion, so that having (d, ε) small generalized influences means that upon applying these Laplacians on F , the
2-norm squared of the resulting function never exceeds ε.
    With this notion in hand, if a function F is (d, ε, t)-pseudo-random, then by [EKL24, Theorem 5.5]
                                  2
we get that F =d has (d, q 500d t ε2 )-small generalized influences. Applying [EKL24, Proposition 3.6] then
                                        2
implies that F =d is (r, q 10dr · q 500d t ε2 )-pseudo-random for any r ⩾ d, which is the desired result.

    Lastly, we need the following global hypercontractivity result also due to [EKL24].10
Theorem A.6. [EKL24, Theorem 1.13] Let t ⩾ 4 be a power of 2 and let F ∈ L2 (Fqn×2ℓ ) be a function of
degree d that is (d, ε)-pseudo-random. Then,

analogue of Lemma 2.3 over the Bilinear Scheme.

A.2    An Analog of Lemma 2.3 for the Bilinear Scheme
In this section, we will build up some Fourier analytic tools over the bilinear scheme towards showing
Lemma 2.3. Specifically, our goal will be to transform the statement from Lemma 2.3 into a statement about
indicator functions over the bilinear scheme, and then apply Theorem A.6.
    To this end, we start with some definitions. Say that a function F ∈ L2 (Fn×2ℓq    ) is basis invariant if it
satisfies F (M ) = F (M A) for any full rank A ∈ Fq   2ℓ×2ℓ . Basis invariant indicator functions are meant to
correspond to indicator functions for sets of subspaces in Grassq (n, 2ℓ). Indeed, by definition, if two M1and
M2 have the same column image, then F (M1 ) = F (M2 ). Define the adjacency operator T : L2 Fn×2ℓ      q      →
                                                                                                              −
                
       n×2(1−δ)ℓ                                                              n×2(1−δ)ℓ
L2 Fq              as follows. For any H ∈ L2 (Fqn×2ℓ ), the function T H : Fq           → C is given by

                                       T F (M ) =            E           [F ([M, v1 , . . . , v2δℓ ])].
                                                       v1 ,...,v2δℓ

Here, [M, v1 , . . . , v2δℓ ] refers to the matrix obtained by adding the columns vi to M on the right, for all
i ∈ [2δℓ]. In words, the operator T averages over extensions of the matrix M to an n × 2ℓ matrix by adding
to it 2δℓ random columns. This operator is morally the adjacency operator for the bipartite Grassmann
graph between Grassq (n, 2ℓ) and Grassq (n, 2(1 − δ)ℓ), and as such, it will ultimately help us transform the
probability of interest in Lemma 2.3, into an inner product over the bilinear scheme.
    Before jumping into Lemma 2.3 though, we must first show some lemmas about basis invariant functions
and the operator T . The first lemma we need is that the level d component of a basis invariant function is
also basis invariant, and to show this, the following two identities will be useful.

The operator Φ is meant to be a version of GT which is easier to work with and acts in the same way on
basis invariant functions. The upshot of working with Φ is that it is an averaging operator with respect to a
Cayley graph over Fn×2ℓ
                      q   , so each character χS is an eigenvector of Φ and the eigenvalues have an explicit
formula (see [Spi19] for a detailed discussion).

    The following lemma gives upper bound on the eigenvalues of Φ.

Lemma A.13. Suppose that rank(S) = t. If t = 0, then χS is an eigenvector of Φ of eigenvalue 1. If t > 0,
χS is an eigenvector of Φ of eigenvalue which is at most 3q t−n + q −t(2δℓ−1) in absolute value.

A.3    Reduction to the Bilinear Scheme
We are now ready to prove Lemma 2.3. Let L ⊆ Grassq (n, 2ℓ) and R ⊆ Grassq (n, 2(1 − δ)ℓ) be as
in Lemma 2.3 and suppose that L is (r, ε)-pseudo-random. Define the related Boolean functions F ∈
                     n×2(1−δ)ℓ
L2 Fqn×2ℓ , H ∈ L2 (Fq
         
                               ) as follows:
                           (                                                               (
                        ′   1        if im(M ′ ) ∈ L,                                       1        if im(M ) ∈ R,
                  F (M ) =                                                         G(M ) =                                               (34)
                            0        otherwise,                                             0        otherwise.

In the above, im(·) is the usual definition of matrix image and refers to the span of the columns of the matrix.
Implicit in the definitions is the fact that F evaluates to 0 if the columns of M ′ are not linearly independent,
as in this case their span is not even a dimension 2ℓ subspace, and likewise H evaluates to 0 if the columns
of M are not linearly independent. We note that F and G are both basis invariant
    Towards applying Lemma A.16, we want to first show that the pseudo-randomness of L carries over
to F . To this end we begin with the following lemma that simplifies the type of zoom-ins and zoom-out
combinations we have to consider when showing F is pseudo-random.

Lemma A.17. For any (U, V ), (X, Y ) such that dim(U ) + codim(X) < 2ℓ and Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] is
nonempty, there are r′ linearly independent columns of V , say v1 , . . . , vr′ ∈ Fnq and a subset of linearly
                                     ′
independent rows of X, say X ′′ ∈ Fsq ×n , such that r′ ⩽ dim(U ), s′ ⩽ codim(X) and
                                     2
                                                                         [F [v1 , . . . , vr′ , M ′ ] | X ′′ M ′ = 0],
                                                                                                     
                    F(U,V ),(X,Y ) 2 ⩽ 2 ·                E
                                                          n×(2ℓ−r ′ )
                                                M ′ ∈Fq


where [v1 , . . . , vr′ , M ′ ] ∈ Fn×2ℓ
                                   q    is the matrix whose first r′ columns are v1 , . . . , vr′ , and remaining columns
are M ′ .




                                                                        80
Proof. Let r = dim(U ) and s = codim(X). First note that we can assume that the columns of U and V
respectively are both nonzero and linearly independent. Indeed, otherwise say ui = 0, then either vi = 0, in
which case the ith columns of U and V can be removed, or vi ̸= 0 and Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] is an empty
set. Otherwise, if, say, vi = 0, then either ui = 0 and again we can ignored the ith columns, or ui ̸= 0 and
Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] consists of matrices whose columns are not linearly independent. In this case F is
identically 0 on Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] and the statement is trivially true. Similarly, if the columns of V are
not linearly independent, then Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] consists of matrices whose columns are not linearly
independent, and again F is identically 0 on Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )]. Finally, if the columns of U are not
linearly independent, then either Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )] is empty or there must be some i such that both ui
and vi are linear combinations of the other columns in U and V respectively, with the same coefficients. In
this case, we can remove the ith columns of U and V without changing Zoom[(U, V ), (X, Y )].
     Now suppose that the columns of U and V are nonzero and linearly independent, and let A ∈ F2ℓ×2ℓ  q     be

B     Proof of Theorem 5.2
In order to prove Theorem 5.2 we will find the subspaces Q one at a time by using Theorem 5.1. We
maintain a list Q of all Q’s collected thus far. Each time a new subspace Q is added to Q, we randomize
the assignment T1 [L] for all 2ℓ-dimensional L ⊃ Q. At a high level, the effect of this randomization is that
there is only a little agreement between any linear function and the assignments on subspaces containing Q,
thus these entries are essentially “deleted”.
    Formally, we construct the set Q of subspaces as follows. Initially set Te1 = T1 , Q = ∅, and X = ∅.
Recall that initially Te1 and T2 are ε-consistent for ε ⩾ 2q −2ℓ(1−1000δ) . While Te1 and T2 are at least ε/2-
consistent, do the following.

    1. Let Q ⊂ W be subspaces guaranteed by Theorem 5.1. That is, Q and W satisfy dim(Q)+codim(W ) =
       r and there exists linear gQ,W : W → Fq such that
                                                                                        2
                           Pr      [gQ,W |L = Te1 [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ] ⩾ ε′ := q −2(1−1000δ )ℓ .
                      L∈Grass(n,2ℓ)


    2. Set Q ←
             − Q ∪ {Q}.

    3. Set X ←
             − X ∪ {L | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W }.

    4. For each L ∈ X independently, choose Te1 [L] uniformly among all linear functions on L.

We have the following claim regarding the re-assignment phase.
                                            ℓn
Claim B.1. With probability at least 1−e−Ω(q ) over the random assignment Te1 , for every pair of subspaces
Q ⊂ W such that dim(Q) + codim(W ) = r and µQ,W (X ) ⩾ q −2ℓ , and every linear function gQ,W : W →      −
Fq , we have
                                       Pr       [gQ,W |L ≡ Te1 [L]] ⩽ q 1−2ℓ
                                L∈X ,L∈Zoom2ℓ [Q,W ]

Proof. Fix Q, W, gQ,W such that dim(Q)+codim(W ) = r and µQ,W (X ) ⩾ q −2ℓ . Let A = Zoom2ℓ [Q, W ]∩
X and for each L ∈ A, let ZL denote the indicator variable that takes value 1 if gQ,W |L = Te1 [L] and 0
otherwise, where Te1 is the randomly chosen assignment over X . Over randomly chosen Te1 , the expectation
of ZL is q −2ℓ , so by a Chernoff bound, we have
                                    "                   #
                                       1 X                     2 −2ℓ
                                Pr           ZL ⩾ q 1−2ℓ ⩽ e−q (q |A|)/6 .
                                 Te   |A|
                                         L∈A

By assumption, |A| ⩾ q −2ℓ |{L ∈ Zoom2ℓ [Q, W ], dim(L) = 2ℓ}| ⩾ q −2ℓ q (2ℓ−r)(n−r−2ℓ) . Thus, using a
union bound over all Q, W, gQ,W , the probability that there exist a bad triple is at most,
                                                   −4ℓ+2 q (2ℓ−r)(n−r−2ℓ) /6             ℓn )
                             (r + 1)q nr q n e−q                               ⩽ e−Ω(q          .

                                                           86
     We now analyze the process. Note that when we find a new triplet (L, Q, gQ,W ), then by Chernoff’s
                                ℓn
bound with probability 1 − e−Ω(q ) over the randomization step, the probability PrL∈Grassq (n,2ℓ) [gQ,W |L =
Te1 [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ] drops from at least ε′ to at most q 1−2ℓ . In that case, the measure of X increases by at
least
                                       (ε′ − q 1−2ℓ )q −rn ⩾ q −O(rn) .
                                                                                    ℓn
Doing a union bound over the steps, it follows that with probability 1 − e−Ω(q ) q O(rn) = 1 − o(1) the
process terminates within q O(rn) steps.
    Note that it is possible that the same subspace Q is added multiple times (with different zoom-outs) in
the process above, so we clarify that Q is considered as a set without repeats. Also note that with probability
1 − o(1), for each Q ∈ Q, W and gQ,W found in the process it holds that

                                                                          1
                                 Pr      [gQ,W |L = T1 [L] | Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ] ⩾ ε′                               (36)
                            L∈Grass(n,2ℓ)                                 2

(the point being is that the agreement now is compared to the original T1 and not to Te1 ). Indeed, considering
the step Q, W and gQ,W were found, gQ,W had agreement at least ε′ with Te1 on Zoom2ℓ [Q, W ] at that point,
                                               ℓn
and by Claim B.1 with probability 1 − e−Ω(q ) at most q 1−2ℓ ⩽ ε′ /2 of that agreement came from L ∈ X .
                                                                          −Ω(ℓn)
Thus, by union bound over all of the steps, with probability 1−q O(rn) eq        = 1−o(1) it follows that (36)
holds for every Q, W and gQ,W found throughout the process.
    The following claim shows that at the end of the process the number of Q’s found in the process is large,
thereby finishing the proof of Theorem 5.2.
                                                                                          2
Claim B.2. There exists some 0 ⩽ r1 ⩽ r such that Q contains at least a q −5ℓ -fraction of all r1 -
dimensional subspaces.

Proof. At the end of the process, the consistency has dropped by at least ε′ /2, so the probability over edges
(L, R) that L was reassigned must be at least ε′ /2. For each 0 ⩽ r1 ⩽ r, let Nr1 be the number of Q of
dimension r1 in Q.
    For each Q of dimension r1 , the fraction of 2ℓ-dimensional L’s that are reassigned due to Q being added
to Q is at most the fraction of 2ℓ-dimensional subspaces that contain Q. This is,
                                              
                                          n
                                       2ℓ − r1 q    q n(2ℓ−r1 )    2
                                               ⩽ 2ℓ(n−2ℓ) = q 4ℓ −r1 n .
                                          n         q
                                          2ℓ q

                                                        2
It follows that there must be some r1 such that Nr1 q 4ℓ −r1 n ⩾ 2(r+1)
                                                                    ε
                                                                        , and rearranging gives that
                                                                     
                                             ε          r1 n    −5ℓ2 n
                                  Nr1 ⩾               q      ⩾q           .
                                        2(r + 1)q 4ℓ2                r1 q

                                                            2
Thus there exists an r1 such that Q contains at least a q −5ℓ -fraction of all r1 -dimensional subspaces.

D      List Decoding Bound
In this section we prove Lemma 5.25, which is a direct consequence of the generic list decoding bound
of [GRS00]:

Theorem D.1. [GRS00, Theorem 15] Let C ⊆ ΣN be a code with alphabet size M := |Σ|, blocklength N ,
and relative distance 1 − γ. Let δ > 0 and R ∈ ΣN . Suppose that C1 , . . . , Cm ∈ ΣN are distinct codewords
from C that each differ from R on at most (1 − δ)-fraction of entries. If
                                              s                     
                                         1              1           1
                                    δ>      +     γ−           1−         ,
                                         M             M            M

then
                                                                  1
                                   m⩽                                                      .
                                          (δ − 1/M )2 − (1 − 1/M )(γ − 1/M )
    Proving Lemma 5.25 is simply a matter of translating to the notation of Theorem D.1, and below are the
details.

for all x1 , . . . , x2ℓ−r1 ∈ W 2ℓ−r1 .
      Note that for distinct v, w ∈ W we have that v · x = w · x for at most 1/q-fraction of x ∈ W . Thus,

C ⩾ q −2(1−ξ)ℓ , and ξ > 0 is the small constant fixed at the start of Section 8 (and recalled at the start of

                                     
Lemma 8.1. There exists a 2 1 − 2ξ ℓ-dimensional subspace X and a linear assignment, σ, to X, such
that the following hold:
      • µX,◦ (LX,σ ) ⩾ C6 .

    For each 2 1 − 2ξ ℓ-dimensional subspace X and linear assignment, σ, to X, let us define

         pX,σ :=            Pr        [L ∈ LX,σ ∧ fi |L ̸= T [L]],      qX,σ :=         Pr           [L ∈ LX,σ ],
                     Wi ∈WX,σ                                                       Wi ∈WX,σ
                   L∈Zoom2ℓ [X,Wi ]                                               L∈Zoom2ℓ [X,Wi ]

where in both probabilities X and σ are fixed, and Wi ∈ WX,σ is chosen uniformly and L ∈ Zoom2ℓ [X, Wi ]
is chosen uniformly. The intention behind these values is that for a fixed (X, σ), the quantity pX,σ should
reflect how much disagreement there is between the table T and the functions fi for Wi ∈ WX,σ , on
subspaces L ∈ LX,σ , while qX,σ should reflect the size of LX,σ . Note that if L ∈ LX,σ and Wi ∈ WX,σ ,
then by definition we already have fi |X ≡ T [L]|X ≡ σ. Therefore we would expect that in fact fi and T [L]
also agree on L (since it is only larger than X by ξℓ dimensions), meaning that we expect pX,σ to typically be
small. Also, for each Wi , there are at least a C-fraction of L ∈ Grass(Wi , 2ℓ) for which fi |L ≡ T [L], so we
would expect qX,σ to be Ω(C) for a non-trivial fraction of (X, σ). In the following claim, we formalize this
intuition and show that there indeed exists an (X, σ) for which pX,σ is small, qX,σ is large, and additionally
the set WX,σ is large.
Remark E.1. The idea of looking for such (X, σ) was first introduced in [IKW12] where they call these
(X, σ)-excellent and was used again in [BDN17, MZ23] to analyze lower dimensional subspace versus
subspace tests, which is similar in spirit to what we are ultimately trying to show in Lemma 5.24.
                                                         

Proof. Set A0 = X, L0 = LX,σ , B0 = V , and η0 = µX,◦ (LX,σ ) ⩾ 12 . Now do the following.

   1. Set i = 0, and initialize A0 , L0 , B0 , η0 as above.

   2. If Li is (1, q δ2 ℓ ηi )-pseudo-random inside of Zoom2ℓ [Ai , Bi ], then stop.

   3. Otherwise, there exist A ⊆ B such that, Ai ⊆ A ⊆ B ⊆ Bi , dim(A) + codim(B) = dim(Ai ) +
      codim(Bi ) + 1, and µA,B (Li ) ⩾ q δ2 ℓ ηi .

   4. Set Ai+1 := A, Bi+1 := B, Li+1 := Li ∩ Zoom2ℓ [Ai+1 , Bi+1 ], and let ηi+1 := µAi+1 ,Bi+1 (Li+1 ).

   5. Increment i by 1 and return to step 2.

satisfies the requirements of the lemma.                                             l              m

E.3.1    A Necessary Fourier Analytic Fact
Let A ⊆ Grass(n, j) and let η = µ(A). We assume that n is sufficiently large relative to q and j and that η

                                    n > q j and η ⩾ 100jq j−n                                          (59)
to be concrete. Define F : Fn×j
                            q   → {0, 1} by

    In the next lemma, we use the characters χS for S = (s1 , . . . , sj ) ∈ Fn×j
                                                                              q   defined as in (31), except we

Lemma E.4. Fix a subspace W ⊆ Fnq , then for any S = (s1 , . . . , sj ) ∈ Fqn×j we have,
                                                                 (

                         x1 ,...,xj ∈W                            0, if S ⊊ W ⊥ .

Proof. If S ⊆ W ⊥ , then for any x ∈ W , we have si · x = 0 for all 1 ⩽ i ⩽ j, so the first case follows.
   Now suppose S ⊊ W ⊥ , and without loss of generality say that s1 ∈  / W ⊥ . We can write,
                                             h           i            h Pj              i
                       E      [χS (x)] = E ω Tr(x1 ·s1 )       E       ω i=2 Tr(xi ·si ) .
                    x1 ,...,xj ∈W            x1 ∈W               x2 ,...,xj ∈W


We will show that Ex1 ∈W ω Tr(x1 ·s1 ) = 0. Notice that it is sufficient to show that x1 · s1 takes each value in
                                     

Fq with equal probability over uniformly random x1 ∈ W . First, since s1 ∈    / W ⊥ , Prx1 ∈W [x1 · s1 = 0] = 1q .
Next note for any α ̸= 0,

                       Pr [x1 · s1 = 1] = Pr [(αx1 ) · s1 = α] = Pr [x1 · s1 = α].
                      x1 ∈W                   x1 ∈W                        x1 ∈W

Therefore, x1 · s1 takes each of the q − 1 nonzero values in Fq with probability 1q over uniform x1 ∈ W , and
this concludes the proof.

   We can now show the necessary fact, asserting that if zooming out to W changes the measure of A
considerably, then we can attribute it to a Fourier character S only containing elements from W ⊥ .

It follows that the number of edges in this graph is an upper bound on the number of bad triples containing
z. Since W ⋆ is 4-generic, we have
                                      (Wi⋆ ∩ Wj⋆ )⊥ ∩ (Wi⋆′ ∩ Wj⋆′ )⊥ = {0}
for all i, j, i′ , j ′ distinct. Therefore, any two neighbours of a vertex on the left must either have their i or j
be equal, and hence the maximum degree of a vertex on the left side is at most 2m3 . As a result, the graph
                                  4sℓ     6 4sℓ
has at most 2m3 · 160000q    η ′2
                                      ⩽ 10 ηq2 m3 edges, where we also use that η ′ ⩾ 0.9η. This completes the proof
of the first assertion of the claim.

Lemma E.8. Let E be any event defined with respect to (z, Wi⋆ , Wj⋆ ). Then D2 (E) ⩽ 2D1 (E).

    We are now ready to prove Lemma 8.5.
Proof of Lemma 8.5. By the fifth property in Lemma 8.3, for every L ∈ L⋆ , we have
                                                 Pr         [fi |L ̸= Tℓ′ [L]] ⩽ 14γ.
